# Contract - forkdrift

This contract is the executable spec for forkdrift. Every obligation below is stated so it can fail. The full contract is the first build act of this repo and it extends the stub obligations written here.

## Stub obligations

FD-S1. Two runs that define the same interface in incompatible ways raise an alarm, and an alarm that does not quote both conflicting definitions is a defect the tests must catch.

FD-S2. Two runs whose definitions of a shared interface agree raise no alarm, and a false alarm on agreeing runs is a defect the tests must catch.

FD-S3. Every alarm names the concept that diverged and the runs that diverged on it, and an alarm missing either is rejected rather than reported incomplete.

FD-S4. Forkdrift detects and reports only, and it never edits a run, a branch, or a definition in order to reconcile a divergence it found.

FD-S5. Every fixture and example in this repo is synthetic and built for this repo, and no real trace or session data appears anywhere in the tree.

## Round 1 obligations

Round 1 defines the artifact format in this contract and builds the reader and the comparison over it. The format is forkdrift's own, so no outside tool behaviour needs probing before the obligations can rest on it. An artifact is a recorded text that one run left behind for one interface, and the comparison runs on two recorded artifacts only and never on a live run.

The format in brief is shown here once, and the obligations below state it as rules that can fail.

```
interface shard-store

replicas = 3
timeout = 30
operation fetch = record
label primary = node-a
excludes = legacy-mode
```

A meaningful line is a line that is neither blank nor a comment, and a comment line is a line whose first character that is not a space is the hash character. The first meaningful line names the interface. Every later meaningful line is a term line, an operation line, a label line, or an excludes line. A term line is a term name, an equals sign, and a value. An operation line is the word operation, an operation name, an equals sign, and a result type. A label line is the word label, a label name, an equals sign, and a value. An excludes line is the word excludes, an equals sign, and a comma separated list of names, and an artifact carries at most one excludes line. Term names, operation names, label names, and interface names are identifiers that start with a letter or an underscore and continue with letters, digits, underscores, dashes, or dots.

FD-1. An artifact parses only when its first meaningful line names exactly one interface and every later meaningful line matches one of the statement forms this contract defines, and a text that breaks that shape is not an artifact and is never compared.

FD-2. A malformed artifact is refused with an error that names the source and the defect, and the refusal covers a missing interface line, a meaningful line that matches no statement form, a duplicate term name or operation name or label name, an empty value, and an artifact that defines a name its own excludes line forbids, and no refusal ever surfaces as a traceback or as a partial report.

FD-3. When both artifacts state the same term and the two values differ after the normalizations named in FD-6, the comparison raises exactly one divergence for that term, and the report quotes the term line from each artifact verbatim and names the source each line came from.

FD-4. When both artifacts state the same operation and the two result types differ after the normalizations named in FD-6, the comparison raises exactly one divergence for that operation, and the report quotes the operation line from each artifact verbatim and names the source each line came from.

FD-5. When one artifact excludes a name and the other artifact defines that name as a term or as an operation, the comparison raises exactly one divergence for that name, and the report quotes the excludes line and the defining line verbatim and names the source each line came from.

FD-6. The only normalizations are the ones named here, namely leading and trailing whitespace is stripped from every line, runs of whitespace inside a value collapse to a single space, and a value that reads as a finite number on both sides compares by numeric worth rather than by its characters, and statement order never matters, and a difference that one of these normalizations removes raises no divergence.

FD-7. Label lines are local annotations that this format declares non-semantic, and a label that is renamed or revalued between the two artifacts raises no divergence, and label names are never dereferenced inside term values or operation result types in this round.

FD-8. A term or an operation stated in only one of the two artifacts raises no divergence in this round unless the other artifact excludes that name, and this limit is a stated limit of the round and is recorded here so a later round can retire it on purpose.

FD-9. Two artifacts that name different interfaces are refused with an error that names both interface names, because the comparison is defined only over two artifacts that describe one and the same interface.

FD-10. The report lists divergences sorted first by interface name and then by the name that diverged and then by divergence kind, and running the comparison twice over the same pair of artifacts produces byte identical reports.

FD-11. The command line tool exits with code 0 when the pair agrees, with code 1 when at least one divergence is found, and with code 2 when either artifact is malformed or the two artifacts name different interfaces or an input file cannot be read, and a refusal prints one error line to the error stream and never a traceback.

FD-12. The exactly one divergence that FD-5 promises holds per excluded name and per direction, and when the other artifact defines the excluded name both as a term and as an operation, the single divergence quotes the excludes line and both defining lines, each with its source named.

FD-13. The lines the report quotes under FD-3, FD-4, and FD-5 are the statement lines after the leading and trailing whitespace strip named in FD-6 has been applied, and the word verbatim in those obligations is read subject to that strip, with all internal spacing preserved exactly.

FD-14. The self-exclusion refusal in FD-2 covers names defined as terms or as operations, and a name carried only as a label is not a definition for that refusal, because FD-7 declares labels non-semantic.

## Round 2 obligations

Round 2 retires the presence limit that FD-8 stated for Round 1 and recorded for a later round to retire on purpose. The comparison now reports the names only one of the two artifacts defines, and the obligations below state the new kind, its interplay with the exclusion rules, and its place in the report order. No other Round 1 obligation changes.

FD-15. The limit stated in FD-8 is retired. A term statement or an operation statement that one artifact makes and the other artifact does not make in the same form is a one-sided statement, and the one-sided statements for one name together raise exactly one divergence of a fourth kind named presence for that name. The report block for a presence divergence follows the row grammar of the blocks Round 1 defined, one left row and one right row and the further row of FD-12 when a side makes one-sided statements in both forms. Each side quotes the one-sided statement lines it makes, the first in its side row and the second in the further row, taken term first and operation second as FD-12 takes defining lines. A side that makes no one-sided statement for the name quotes its interface line on its side of the block, so every row of the block quotes a line the named source states. A name that both artifacts state in the same form raises no presence divergence for that form, and when the values in that form differ after the normalizations of FD-6 the divergence of FD-3 or FD-4 is the one raised for that form. The kind presence is a new value in the block header and no new row shape is introduced, so a reader of the Round 1 report format meets a kind value it can name in a grammar it already parses.

FD-16. The presence kind and the exclusion contradiction never both fire for one name. When one artifact excludes a name and the other artifact defines that name, the only divergence raised for that name is the exclusion contradiction of FD-5 as FD-12 amends it, and no presence divergence is raised for that name in either direction. A name that one artifact excludes and neither artifact defines raises no divergence, and a name that both artifacts exclude raises no divergence. Labels raise no presence divergence, because FD-7 declares label lines non-semantic. The self-exclusion refusal of FD-2 as FD-14 reads it is unchanged, and an artifact that defines a name its own excludes line forbids is refused and never compared.

FD-17. The presence kind stands in the ordering of FD-10 on the same terms as the kinds Round 1 defined, so the report lists divergences sorted first by interface name and then by the name that diverged and then by divergence kind, with a presence divergence placed by its interface, its name, and its kind. The exit codes of FD-11 are unchanged, and a presence divergence counts as a divergence under that rule, so a pair whose only divergences are presence divergences exits with code 1 and a pair with no divergence of any kind exits with code 0.

FD-18. Amendment, added by the Round 2 review. Two corrections. First, the further row a block carries when one side defines a name both ways is labelled for the side its line comes from, left or right as the case may be, in exclusion blocks as in presence blocks; the rendering before this amendment labelled every further row right, which contradicted the source it named whenever the left side defined the name both ways, and the Round 1 reports whose further rows come from the right side are unchanged byte for byte. Second, FD-15's reader sentence is restated to the strength the evidence supports: the presence kind changes no row's shape, and a report carrying it is written in the grammar Round 1 defined, but a reader that admits only the Round 1 kind values must still be taught the new value before it can read such a report, and teaching crosscurrent's parser is that repo's own round's work.

## Round 3 obligations

Round 3 states the grammar of a number, which FD-6 named by worth without ever stating which values read as numbers, so the question fell to the string parser of the implementation and a Python source literal convention stood inside recorded data unnamed. The amendment below states the grammar as a grammar of this format, and records two readings the earlier rounds left unnamed. No other obligation changes. The behaviour that changes is the two cases the amendment names, the underscore case and the digit characters of other scripts.

FD-19. Amendment, added in Round 3. A number in this format is an optional sign, then one or more digits with an optional fraction, or a fraction standing alone, then an optional exponent. A fraction is a point followed by zero or more digits when digits stand before the point, so 5. reads as the number five, and a point followed by one or more digits when no digits stand before it, so .5 reads as the number five tenths. An exponent is the letter e or E, an optional sign, and one or more digits. An underscore never stands inside a number, and the spellings of infinity and of not a number are never numbers. The digits of this grammar are the ASCII digits 0 to 9, and a digit character from another script is not a digit of this format, so such a value compares by its characters where the implementation's parser once read it as a number. Two values that both match this grammar compare by exact decimal worth under FD-6, so integers of any length compare exactly and decimal fractions compare digit for digit past the point. The worth comparison is carried out on the number's own digits and exponent, so it is exact at any exponent the grammar admits, and two spellings of one number agree however large the exponent stands. A value that does not match this grammar compares by its normalized characters under FD-6, so 1_000 against 1000 raises the term value divergence of FD-3 where the two agreed before this amendment, and 1_000 against 1_000 still agrees. Two readings stand recorded here as readings and not as new behaviour. A name repeated inside one excludes list is inert, because the list is a set of names and a repetition adds nothing to it. The refusals for a second excludes line, an empty excludes list, a trailing comma in an excludes list, and a repeated interface line stand exactly as FD-1 and FD-2 already produce them.

## Round 4 obligations

Round 4 gives forkdrift the sweep. The comparison of Rounds 1 to 3 runs on exactly two recorded artifacts, and a fleet of runs leaves more than two tapes behind, so the sweep compares one baseline artifact against each of several others in turn and prints one document that attributes every divergence to the pair that raised it. The pairwise comparison itself does not change, and the sweep document is a new document kind that stands beside the pairwise report and never replaces it.

FD-20. A sweep compares one baseline artifact against each of several other artifacts in turn, in the order the others are given, and every comparison in a sweep is exactly the pairwise comparison of FD-3 to FD-19 over the same two artifacts, with the same parser, the same divergences, and the interface refusal of FD-9 applied pair by pair. The library entry point sweep_artifacts takes the baseline and the others and returns one divergence list per other artifact, in the order the others were given, and a pair that refuses refuses the whole sweep with the refusal that pair raises, so no partial result stands in for a sweep one of whose pairs could not be compared.

FD-21. The sweep document prints, for every pair in order, one header line and then that pair's report in the grammar of FD-3 to FD-19, and the report of a pair that agrees is empty, so an agreeing pair contributes its header line alone and a sweep in which every pair agrees prints exactly its header lines. The header line reads pair left, then the baseline source in parentheses, then right, then the other source in parentheses, in the form pair left (SOURCE) right (SOURCE) with the two source names standing where the capital words stand, and the line is built from the two source names and the fixed words pair and left and right alone. The header line stands outside the block grammar of the pairwise report, because a block opens with the word divergence and a row opens with two spaces and a side word, and the header opens with the word pair at the start of the line, so no reader of the pairwise grammar can take a header line for a block or for a row, and the sweep document is never a pairwise report and is never fed to a reader of pairwise reports.

FD-22. The command line gains a sweep form beside compare, and compare keeps its exact two argument shape and its behaviour in every byte. The sweep form takes one baseline argument and one or more other arguments, and a sweep asked for with no other artifact is a usage error that the argument parser reports on the error stream with exit code 2 before any file is read, and no document is printed. A sweep over files reads and parses the baseline before any pair is compared, and then takes the others in the order given. The sweep exits with code 0 when every pair agrees, with code 1 when at least one pair diverges, and with code 2 when any pair refuses, and a refusal in any pair refuses the whole sweep in the discipline of FD-11 and FD-2, one error line on the error stream naming the refusal that pair raises, no document and no partial document on the output stream, and never a traceback, and the refusal reported is the first refusal in pair order.

FD-23. The pairwise report grammar and the compare command are unchanged in every byte, and the committed pairwise example documents stand exactly as Rounds 1 to 3 left them. The committed examples gain one sweep document, produced by running the tool itself over the committed tapes with examples/run-a.tape as the baseline and examples/run-b.tape and examples/presence-a.tape and examples/presence-b.tape as the others in that order, and the document is pinned byte for byte the way the pairwise example documents are pinned.

## Round 5 obligations

Round 5 ports forkdrift from Python to a native Rust crate. The format, the comparison, the report grammar, and the sweep do not change, and the obligations below state the crate shape, the invariants in the crate's own form, and the print and argument disciplines the new runtime must reproduce. The readings the port rests on were probed against the Python implementation and its runtime before the build, and the probe corrections after the obligations record the places where the probes corrected the reading of an earlier obligation. Forkdrift still detects semantic divergence only, and nothing in this port performs a reconciliation.

FD-24. The tool is a native Rust crate. Its manifest declares the package forkdrift on edition 2024 with a library target and a binary target, clap 4 with the derive feature and thiserror 2 as its dependencies, anyhow 1 confined to the binary entry point, and sha2 and serde_json as development dependencies, sha2 for the setup tests and serde_json for the generated command line corpus alone. The shipped library and binary carry no serde dependency and read and write no JSON, because forkdrift's data is recorded text in the grammar of FD-1 and reports in the grammar of FD-2 and FD-21.

FD-25. Every obligation from FD-1 through FD-23 holds in the Rust implementation with unchanged meaning, and the Python test corpus is ported test for test into Rust tests under the tests directory that keep the corpus file names, so every behaviour the Python suite pinned stays pinned. Where an earlier obligation names the Python implementation, its test runner, or its module layout, the crate's own form of the same check stands in its place, and FD-26 states the setup invariants in the crate's own form.

FD-26. The setup invariants hold in the crate's own form. The canonical block of the agent instructions file keeps the digest it carries today, measured over the text above the repo annex heading, and the annex keeps naming this contract. The stub obligations of the first slice stand in this contract exactly as written. The readme keeps exactly one closed rounds count of the pinned form, and its status line keeps reporting the rounds this contract has closed. No tracked text file in the repo contains an em dash or an en dash, and the scan covers the extensionless records and the manifest files as well as the suffixed ones.

FD-27. The committed example documents stay the documents the tool itself produces, and the Rust binary regenerates the pairwise reports of FD-23 and the sweep document of FD-23 byte for byte, so the example tests that pin them keep passing against the port with no byte changing in any committed example file.

FD-28. The worth comparison of FD-6 and FD-19 is carried out on the number's own digit string and a signed exponent held at arbitrary precision, so it stays exact at any exponent the grammar admits, subject only to the conversion limit the probe correction to FD-19 records. The reader's text model is the oracle's own. Lines split on the oracle's boundary set with the carriage return and line feed pair counting as one boundary, and whitespace strips and collapses over the oracle's whitespace set, and both sets stand in the implementation as tables taken from the oracle runtime's own character data. The port carries discriminating pins for both tables, a behaviour pin for every boundary character and for the edges of every whitespace range, so deleting any entry from either table breaks a test.

FD-29. The implementation states its ceilings where the oracle has them and adds none of its own. The mantissa digit string is never converted to a number at any length, so integers of any length compare exactly, and a text of any size parses line by line with line numbers counted over the boundary set of FD-28. The one ceiling the oracle does hold, the exponent digit conversion limit, stands in the probe correction to FD-19, and the one divide the print discipline holds stands in FD-30.

FD-30. When the document cannot be printed because the reader of standard output is already gone, the command answers with status 120 and one line of its own on the error stream when the whole document is shorter than 8192 bytes, because the oracle's write stays buffered and the failure surfaces only at the exit flush, and with status 1 and one line of its own when the document runs to 8192 bytes or more, because the oracle's buffer empties inside the write and the failure escapes the command uncaught. Printed help follows the short case. A refusal line on the error stream is written plainly, and the status stands whether or not that write succeeds.

FD-31. The Python implementation, its tests, its project file, and its test configuration are removed in the same slice that lands the port, after the differential run against the Python implementation passes, and the tracked tree then holds no Python source. The continuous integration backstop runs the crate's test suite and nothing else.

FD-32. The command line keeps every functional outcome of FD-10, FD-11, and FD-22, meaning the exit statuses, the files read, and the contents of both streams. The argument presentation is the Rust argument parser's own, so help wording and malformed invocation wording follow the parser while no functional outcome follows it. The argument scan keeps the readings the probes established before the build, and the probe correction to FD-11 records them.

Probe correction to FD-2, recorded in the Round 5 contract pass before the build and governing where the two disagree. A report prints each source with the exact bytes the path was given in, so a path whose bytes are not valid UTF-8 stands in a report with those bytes unaltered, because the oracle decodes its arguments with the surrogate escape handler and encodes its report the same way. A refusal line names the same source with every undecodable byte written in the escaped form \udcXX, a backslash and the letters u and d and c and the byte's two lowercase hex digits standing where XX stands, because the oracle's error stream replaces what it cannot encode. The probe ran both shapes against a file whose name carries the byte 0xff and recorded the report line carrying the raw byte and the refusal line carrying the escaped form, and both shapes stand corrected here.

Probe correction to FD-10, recorded in the Round 5 contract pass before the build and governing where the two disagree. The name in the cannot read line is the oracle path library's folded form of the path as written and not the path as written itself. Empty components and single dot components fall away, dot dot components stay, a leading pair of slashes stays a pair while any longer run of leading slashes folds to one, and a path left with no components names a single dot, so an empty path argument reads as the current directory and fails with the directory wording against the name dot, and a directory named with a trailing slash fails against its folded name. Reports and parse refusals keep naming the path as written, and only the read refusal folds. The probe recorded the folded names for dotted, doubled, rooted, and empty paths against the oracle before the build.

Probe correction to FD-11, recorded in the Round 5 contract pass before the build and governing where the two disagree. The argument readings are the oracle parser's own and three of them are easy to get wrong. A separator token standing before any subcommand is itself read as the subcommand candidate, so it refuses as an invalid choice at status 2 and it blocks a help token after it the way any invalid candidate does, while inside a subcommand the separator hands the tokens after it to the command as values and a second separator token is itself a value. A token carrying a space is read as a path value whatever its prefix, and the help reading is taken before the space reading, so the token -h x asks for help while the token -- a.tape names a path. A token naming a negative number in the oracle's pattern is a path value, and the pattern reads a trailing newline as the end of the token, so -1 followed by a newline reads as the number while -1. and -1e3 do not read as numbers at all. A help token fires in any position up to the value that blocks it, and a help token written with an equals sign and a value refuses at status 2.

Probe correction to FD-19, recorded in the Round 5 contract pass before the build and governing where the two disagree. The exponent conversion refuses an exponent written with more than 4300 digits, and the refusal escapes the command uncaught at status 1 with nothing on the output stream, in a comparison and in a sweep alike, and the sweep prints no partial document. A zero mantissa rescues nothing, because the conversion runs before the zero check, and a value compared against its own exact text still agrees without any conversion taking place. The probe recorded the boundary at the oracle, 4300 digits converting and 4301 refusing, and the port answers the refusing shapes at status 1 with one line of its own on the error stream where the oracle prints a traceback.

Probe correction to FD-30, recorded in the Round 5 review pass after the build and governing where the two disagree. The closing sentence of FD-30 pictures the refusal status standing whether or not the error stream write succeeds, and that reading rested on probes of a dead standard output alone, because the pre-build probes never closed the error stream. Probed in the review pass with the reading end of the error stream closed before the command starts, the oracle answers 120 for a refusal and 120 for a usage error alike, because the line stays in the buffer and the failure surfaces at the exit flush, the same shape FD-30 records for a short document on a dead standard output. The port answers the same, so a refusal line or a parser error line that cannot be written to the error stream answers 120, and a line that lands keeps the status FD-11 and FD-32 already give it. The closing sentence of FD-30 stands corrected on this point.

## Round 6 - the source-name line-break refusal

Round 6 closes the Minor the Round 4 red team raised and the Round 5 closure carried on its books. A source name is an input-side string the tool prints as it was given. The report FD-3 through FD-5 describe quotes the name around the line it attributes, and the sweep header of FD-21 names both artifacts of a pair, and until this round nothing read the name before printing it, so a name carrying a line break spliced itself into the document as structure. A crafted name could end a quoted row early and stand a whole forged row inside a genuine divergence block, and no reader of the report could tell the forged row from a real one.

The probes for this round stand behind the obligations below. Every site a source name reaches print was read in the code and exercised through the real command line on the base crate, and a name built to forge a row did forge one. The refusal voice is the one the repo already speaks for input-side defects, the FD-2 and FD-9 family at status 2 with one line on the error stream. Two committed corpus cases carry a line feed inside an argv token, and in neither case does the token become the source name of a comparison, one firing the help text and the other standing in the FD-10 read refusal of a file that does not exist, so no committed expectation changes meaning under the refusal and both cases stand pinned as they are.

FD-33. A comparison refuses a source name that carries a line break, and the refusal is raised by the comparison itself ahead of the interface check of FD-9 and ahead of any divergence the comparison computes, the left name read before the right. The line breaks are the characters of the line boundary set FD-28 fixes in the text model, read in the name as written, the seven ASCII members as single bytes, which never stand inside a multi-byte sequence, and U+0085, U+2028, and U+2029 by the UTF-8 byte sequences that encode them, so a name that is not valid UTF-8 is read on the same terms a report would print it, and a byte outside those characters and sequences, a lone continuation byte among them, is not a line break. The refusal is a ForkdriftError of the refusal family, its text naming the side and the defect and interpolating no part of the name it refuses, reading "the left source name carries a line break" for the left name and "the right source name carries a line break" for the right.

FD-34. The refusal stands at every surface that would print the name, covering the pairwise comparison and the sweep of FD-20 in their library and command line forms alike, so no report and no sweep document prints a source name that carries a line break, because no comparison that would print one returns a document at all. A sweep raises the refusal from the first pair whose comparison carries the name, in the pair order FD-22 fixes, and in a sweep the baseline stands as the left name of every pair under FD-21, so a baseline name that carries a line break refuses the sweep at its first pair, ahead of any later pair being read on the command line. A sweep that refuses prints no part of its document and a comparison that refuses prints no report, and the command line answers the refusal in the form FD-11 states, status 2 with no output on the standard stream.

FD-35. FD-3, FD-4, FD-5, and FD-21 stand amended for a source name that carries a line break and stand as written for every other name. The report rows and the pair header quote such a name nowhere, because the comparison refuses it first, and the interface refusal of FD-9 never names one either, the name check standing ahead of it under FD-33. A name with no line break is untouched by this section, and spaces, tabs, quotation marks, and punctuation in a name print exactly as they printed before this round, a tab being whitespace in FD-28's model and not a boundary character. The read refusals of FD-10 and FD-11 and the parse refusals of FD-2 keep the forms and the naming they already have, a name that cannot be read or parsed never reaching a comparison to be refused by one, and the worth raise of FD-6 and the print family of FD-30 are unchanged.

## Round 7 - the README rebuild and the one description

Round 7 rebuilds the README to the fleet README standard and aligns the repo's one description text on every surface that carries it. It changes no behavior and amends no boundary. The description stands in four variants at the base of this round, on the README lede and on the package description and on the crate doc line and on the command line about, and the census recorded before this round stands behind the obligations below with the four standings in it.

The one description text is the repo's GitHub description, word for word. Tells whether two recorded artifacts that state the same interface actually diverge. Both files are read, compared structurally, and reported without executing either. The command line about is set at the base, to the package variant with a final period added, so setting the about to the one text replaces the about line the root help prints, and FD-37 scopes that replacement as the round's one designed difference.

The carried red team note from Round 4, that FD-10 answers before the FD-33 name refusal when a sweep's first counterpart is unreadable, stands adjudicated by FD-35, which states that a name that cannot be read or parsed never reaches a comparison to be refused by one. This round builds nothing on it and records the adjudication here.

FD-36. The README is rebuilt to the fleet README standard. Its first screen carries the title and a one line lede and one short paragraph and one honest badge set and one block of real captured output, in that order, and Install and Quickstart and Usage and Status and License follow. The one description text stands identically as the README lede and as the package description in Cargo.toml and as the crate doc line in src/lib.rs and as the command line's about text in src/main.rs, and no variant of it stands anywhere in the tree. Every captured output block in the README is the head binary's real output on the committed examples, captured from a run and never composed. The badge set is the CI badge for the gate workflow and the MIT license badge, placed once.

FD-37. Behavior invariance, scoped. The round touches prose and doc comments and the about text and nothing else, and the description move of FD-36 replaces the about line of the root help by design, because the about stands set to a variant at the base. The proof is the full suite at the head and a base versus head binary differential over the committed corpus plus help and error surfaces, with no difference accepted anywhere except root help surfaces whose about line is replaced by the one description text over an otherwise unchanged remainder. Moved corpus expectations are confined to that replacement, proven by parsed structure diff, and the full corpus re-runs against the final binary. Any other difference is a defect and stops the round.

FD-38. Status discipline. The slice keeps the README status at Closed rounds 6 of 100 and the setup pin at its current count, and the round count moves only in the closure change after review. The description of FD-36 carries a discriminating content pin in the setup suite in this same slice, asserting the one text on the README lede and the package description and the crate doc line and, through the corpus root help expectations, the command line help surface.

## Round 8 - the root long set, guarded

Round 8 discharges the note the port round left on its books, that the preprocess walk reads help tokens on the root against a hand written long set where the subcommand sets are derived. The walk stands on the parser the scan has reached, and for a subcommand the table comes from the built command through subcommand_specs, which collects the long of every argument the derive model declares and completes the set with the help long where it stands absent. For the root the walk holds a vector written by hand inside preprocess, and the vector holds --help alone.

The probes recorded here stand behind the obligations of this round, and the root command was probed through the base binary before any of them was written. The version long refuses at status 2 as an unexpected argument, because the root declares no version. The help long and its prefixes --hel and --he and --h and the short form -h each fire the root help at status 0. A help token carrying a value refuses at status 2 in the voice of the underlying parser, and the unknown longs --vers and --unknown refuse at status 2. The committed corpus pins those root help classes already, in the cases named help-long and help-short and help-prefix-he and help-prefix-h and help-value-long, so the surface of the root help reading needs no new pin in this round.

What no standing test reaches is the table itself. The hand set is correct at the base and nothing guards it, so a root long added to the command in a later round would leave every standing test green while the hand set stayed at the one help long, and the abbreviation reading of the walk would then read help tokens against a parser that no longer stands there. This round states the invariant and pins the hand set to the command it describes.

FD-39. The root parser declares no long flag but --help, and the preprocess walk reads help tokens on the root against a long set that holds --help alone for that reason and for no other. The hand set stands guarded by a pin in the test suite that derives the root long set from the built command in the same manner subcommand_specs derives the long set of a subcommand, collecting the long of every argument the root command carries and completing the set with the help long where it stands absent, and asserts the derived set is exactly the one help long. A long flag added to the root command, declared as an argument or raised by an attribute, breaks the pin, so no added long can leave the hand set standing behind the parser in silence. The guard runs one way by construction, because the derivation completes the set with the help long where it stands absent, and the help long's own presence on the root stands pinned by the corpus help cases rather than by this pin.

FD-40. Behavior invariance, scoped. ## Round 9 - the root help short, guarded

Round 9 closes the note every review of the Round 8 guard has recorded since, in this repo and in the transfers of that guard to thirdshift and cavalry alike. The preprocess walk reads short help tokens against a letter written by hand in two places, and nothing derives or pins that the help flag of the parser carries that letter. FD-39 guarded the long set the walk reads on the root, and the short letter the two short reads test stood outside that guard, because the derivation of FD-39 collects longs and never shorts.

The two reads are reads_as_positional and help_read. The first tests the second character of a dash token against the letter h ahead of its space rule, so a token of the short help family never reads as a positional, and the second fires or refuses a short help token on the same letter and on the continuation that follows it. The letter stands because the help argument of the root command carries the short h. That argument is no declared argument of the derive model. It is the auto argument the parser raises on the built command, the same materialization the derivation of FD-39 relies on when it collects the help long from the built command rather than from the declared arguments. The root command sets no help flag option in its attribute block, no disable and no custom short, so the auto argument carries the short the parser gives it by default.

If the short of the help flag were ever customized, the two reads would go on testing tokens against h while the parser fired help on another letter, and the walk would read help tokens against a parser that no longer stands there, the same silent drift FD-39 closed for the long set. If the help flag were disabled outright, the reads would stand ready to fire a help the parser cannot give. The committed corpus pins the short help behaviour of the walk already, in the root help cases named help-short and help-prefix-h and help-bundle-hx and help-bundle-hh among others, so the behavioural presence of the letter needs no new pin in this round. What no standing test reaches is the fact the two reads rest on, that the help argument of the built root command carries the short h. This round states that fact and pins it.

FD-42. The two reads that test the second character of a token against the letter h, the short help test of reads_as_positional and the short help branch of help_read, test that letter because the help argument of the root command carries the short h, and for no other reason. That fact stands guarded by a pin in the same test module as the pin of FD-39, and the pin takes the built root command in the manner of that derivation, finds the argument whose long is the help long, and asserts that the short of that argument is exactly the letter h. A help short customized to another letter breaks the pin, and a root command carrying no help argument, its help flag disabled, breaks the pin, so neither change can leave the two reads standing behind the parser in silence. The guard is bounded as the guard of FD-39 is bounded, and it pins the letter the reads use, and the firing and refusing behaviour of the reads on real tokens stands pinned by the corpus help cases rather than by this pin.

## Round 11 - the fold, stated

Round 11 states the fold the maintainer ruled on 2026-11-08, the program's one consolidation. Crosscurrent folds into forkdrift as a reconcile subcommand, one workflow and one repo. Forkdrift detects semantic divergence between two recorded artifacts and prints a divergence record, and crosscurrent reads that record with two recorded branch states and does the session work over it, staging the cross-apply and printing the handback of sized reconciliation tasks. This round writes the fold into this contract as obligations the build round that follows must meet. It changes no code and no behaviour, and it closes no part of the fold.

The census recorded before this round stands behind the obligations below. It groups the standing obligations of crosscurrent, XC-1 to XC-39 as that contract stands at its Round 7 closure, by what the reconcile subcommand must carry, and it names the eight articles that state the closing repo's own house and do not carry. It states forkdrift's two hand-off formats as they stand, the divergence record this repo prints and the branch-state format crosscurrent defines over this repo's artifact forms. It records the command line crosscurrent stands on today, the two subcommands and their argument shapes, that the subcommand's work must reproduce. And it names four carried items from crosscurrent's Round 7 record as inputs to the build round, the canonical-key double clone of XC-34, the set wording of XC-33 against its canonical-sequence key, the port review's note on the phrasing of XC-28, and the live-branch framing note of crosscurrent's agent file annex, that last one belonging to crosscurrent's closing round and not to the build.

FD-49. Forkdrift gains a subcommand named reconcile that performs the session work crosscurrent performs today. The subcommand does the session's two pieces of work in the command forms the census records, printing the cross-apply report over two recorded branch states and one recorded divergence record, and printing the handback of sized reconciliation tasks over the two recorded branch states and a bundle of one or more recorded divergence records. The specification of the subcommand is the carried set of crosscurrent's obligations that FD-50 names, standing as those articles stand in crosscurrent's contract at its Round 7 closure. When the fold is verified complete, the crosscurrent repository closes, and its contract stands unedited as the historical record of the tool it was. The boundary articles hold through the fold. Forkdrift detects semantic divergence only, over recorded artifacts, and the reconcile subcommand performs no detection and takes over none. The subcommand never verifies a record against the branch states or against the world and never re-derives a divergence the record does not carry, the trust limit of crosscurrent's XC-14 standing as a limit on the subcommand itself. Forkdrift edits no run, no branch, and no definition in order to reconcile a divergence, in the subcommand as in the detector, under FD-S4.

FD-50. The carried set is named here by its XC numbers in five groups, and the build round implements the subcommand to those articles as they stand in crosscurrent's contract. The branch-state grammar group is XC-1 and XC-2. The record reader group is XC-3, XC-4, XC-15, XC-18, and XC-19. The reconciliation and staging group is XC-5, XC-6, XC-7, XC-8, XC-17, XC-27, XC-28, and XC-35. The handback group is XC-9, XC-10, XC-11, XC-12, XC-16, XC-20, XC-21, XC-22, XC-29, XC-30, XC-32, XC-33, XC-34, and XC-36. The trust limit group is XC-13 and XC-14. XC-23 does not carry because it declares that repo's own crate and manifest and the subcommand stands in this repo's crate under FD-24, and XC-24 does not carry because it states the carried articles hold in that repo's own port and names that repo's test corpus and the build round's porting standard stands in FD-52 instead, and XC-25 does not carry because it states that repo's own setup invariants over its agent file digest and its readme count and its stub wording, and XC-26 does not carry because it pins that repo's committed example documents and the provenance sentence of its readme status and its behavioural substance stands restated as the differential standard of FD-52, and XC-31 does not carry because it orders the removal of that repo's own Python tree in its port slice, and XC-37 does not carry because it rebuilds that repo's readme and aligns that repo's one description, and XC-38 does not carry because it scopes the invariance of that readme slice, and XC-39 does not carry because it states the status discipline of that repo's own rounds. ## Round 15 - the preprocess walk pinned branch by branch

Round 15 closes the branch layer of this repo's argument walk. The walk stands in preprocess in src/main.rs, and at the base its branches stand pinned only where the committed corpora happen to fire them and where the help guards of FD-39 to FD-48 pin the tables the walk reads. The sibling repos closed this layer one by one, cuesheet in its Rounds 9 and 10 over its scan surface, badbatch in Round 12 over scan, thirdshift in Round 12 and westering in Round 11 over their preprocess walks, and odradek in Round 12 over its scan. This round closes it here, on the same model, a census of the walk's branches from its own code, direct unit pins over the walk's outcomes in the test module of src/main.rs, and one mutation per family stated as a requirement whose executed set corrects the obligation text inside the round when execution falsifies it. Every expectation in this round derives from this walk's code and its governing articles, and no expectation is taken from a sibling's pattern, because the branch truth of a walk is its own. The census recorded before this round stands behind the obligations below.

One fact shapes the census and separates this walk from its siblings. Forkdrift declares no long flag of its own on any command, so every table the walk reads holds the help long alone, the root table from the hand function root_longs and the command and form tables derived from the built command and completed with the help long where it stands absent. The tree carries no value flag at all, so this walk has no pending value arm of the kind the sibling walks carry, and its branch families are its own.

FD-69. The census. The walk's branches stand in seven families, and this obligation states each family with its governing article and the pins it takes, before any pin is written. The first family is the help reads, the long arm and the short arm of help_read as they fire and refuse on every level the walk reaches, the root and compare and sweep and reconcile and the two reconcile forms, governed by the probe correction to FD-11, which records that a help token fires in any position up to the value that blocks it and that a help token written with a value refuses. The family takes fifteen pins, among them the long prefix firing and refusing with a value at the root, the short bundle firing, the short token carrying a value refusing in the rewritten form the walk hands to the parser, the help path naming the level the walk stands on at each of the six commands, and a help token firing ahead of a value already gathered. The second family is the slot recognition, the exact name match that fills the subcommand slot and the form slot and the blocking that answers a token neither slot can take, governed by FD-32 and the probe correction to FD-11, which records that a separator standing before any subcommand is read as the subcommand candidate itself. The family takes seven pins, among them an unknown word blocking at the root and behind reconcile, a mere prefix of a command name blocking because the match is exact, a form name blocking at the root where no form slot stands, and an option token at either slot collected while the walk goes on. The third family is the classification reads of reads_as_positional and the negative number pattern it rests on, governed by the probe correction to FD-11, which records the space reading and the negative number reading and the trailing newline the pattern forgives. The family takes thirteen pins, among them the space token reading as a value behind a command and blocking at the root, the negative spellings that read as values, the trailing dot and exponent spellings that read as options, the lone dash, the equals token whose name is too short for the long arm, and two direct pins over the classification read itself, the help family letter standing ahead of the space rule and the decimal digits of another script reading as a negative number. The fourth family is the separator arms, governed by the probe correction to FD-11 for the detector levels and by the probe corrections to XC-12 and XC-22 as FD-50 carries them for the form levels. The family takes nine pins, a separator blocking at the root and behind reconcile before any form stands on a tail that carries tokens and on an empty tail alike, a separator behind a command handing the tokens after it over as values, and a second separator standing as a value itself. The fifth family is the gathered handoff the walk builds when no distribution runs, the values gathered in order behind one inserted separator and the collected options standing ahead of it in theirs, governed by FD-32. The family takes four pins, among them the handoff carrying no separator when no value was gathered and the empty stream running as an empty handoff. The sixth family is the reconcile routing and the distribution gate, the form slot filling from the token behind reconcile and the gate that admits the tokens behind a form to the distribution only when no option token stands collected on any level and the walk stands unblocked, governed by FD-49 and FD-54. The family takes seven pins, among them one veto pin for an option token collected at each of the three levels, a form asked for too few values and a stage form asked for one value too many, both keeping the gathered handoff. The seventh family is the distribution itself, the assignment of the tokens behind a form across its positional actions under the probe corrections to XC-12 and XC-22, governed by those corrections as FD-50 carries them. The family takes twelve pins, among them the separator joining the share of the value before it at each position it can stand, a second separator leaving the share it joins empty so the slot answers as a list in the stage form and in the handback form alike, trailing separators leaving the handback bundle empty, leading separators leaving one separator standing as the left value, and the space token and the negative number filling a slot as plain arguments. Four bounds stand derived in the census and take no pin. The long arm's multiple match branch is unreachable, because every table the walk reads holds one long, so a prefix match here is never ambiguous and the match arm's help check is true whenever a match stands. The distribution's plain argument head check is jointly covered for option shaped tokens, because the same tokens are collected as options in the walk and veto the gate before the distribution is reached, and the check's independent reach stands only over tokens the walk already gathered. A listed left slot in a distribution is unreachable, because the first share always holds the first argument token once the separator deletion has run. And the form option veto of the gate is jointly covered by the distribution's head check, a bound the gate mutation of FD-71 demonstrates by leaving that one veto pin standing while its two sibling veto pins fall. And the separator arms coincide on one shape and separate on another, so that shape takes pins and no bound. Where a separator stands at the root or behind reconcile before any form stands and tokens follow it, no value can stand gathered ahead of it, so the separated reading's gathered handoff reproduces the blocked raw stream token for token, and the fourth family's two blocking pins on such tails pin that outcome and not the arm. Where the separator stands last and its tail is empty, the gathered handoff inserts no separator of its own, so the separated reading drops the separator the blocked reading keeps and the arms separate token for token, and the fourth family's empty tail pins pin that separation.

FD-70. The pins. Sixty-seven direct unit pins stand in the test module of src/main.rs over the outcomes of preprocess, one for each branch shape the census of FD-69 names, and two of them stand over the classification read the walk rests on where a shape cannot be isolated through the walk's outcomes alone. Each pin asserts the whole outcome the walk answers, the handed over stream token for token for a run, the command path for a fired help, and the assigned slots and bundle for a distribution, so a pin fails when any part of its branch's answer moves. Each expectation was derived from its governing article before the pin ran, and an expectation copied from the walk's own output is no expectation. Where a valued help form's refusal and a plain no help reading of the same token produce the same handed over stream, the pin's stream carries a following help token, so the refusal's block is the only reading under which the stream stands, on the fleet's standing lesson that a pin whose block cannot fail is a defect. A pin whose assertion a neighbouring guard or a standing corpus case already makes in the same terms is scope creep, and a pin that cannot fail for a real reason is a defect, and either stops the round.

FD-71. The mutations. The fourth mutation makes the negative number pattern match nothing, and it breaks the four negative value pins of the third family, the direct digits pin, and the distribution pin whose slot the negative number fills, and no other pin of this round. The fifth mutation as first stated made a separator block at no level, handing the tokens after it over as values everywhere, and it was executed over the pins as first written and broke none of them, the two readings coinciding at the outcome on every stream those pins stage, and over the round's final pin set it breaks exactly the four empty tail pins of the fourth family, whose separation the review's probes proved by execution, so the mutation stands restated as the swap of the separator arms, a separator behind a command or a form blocking instead of handing the tokens after it over as values, and it breaks seventeen pins, the nine separator carrying distribution pins of the seventh family, the pin of the sixth family whose two separators behind a stage form keep the gathered handoff, the three handing over pins of the fourth family, and the four empty tail pins of the fourth family, and no other pin of this round. The sixth mutation drops the separator the gathered handoff inserts ahead of its values, and it breaks every pin of this round whose expected stream carries that inserted separator, the gathered pins of the second and third and fifth and sixth families, the three handing over pins of the fourth family, and the gathered fallbacks of the seventh, and no pin whose answer is a fired help or a distribution or a blocked stream. The seventh mutation removes the separator deletion from the distribution's shares, and it breaks the nine separator carrying distribution pins of the seventh family and no other pin of this round. The eighth mutation collects a positional candidate as an option at both command slots in place of blocking on it, and it breaks the five blocking pins of the second family and the space token's block at the root, and no other pin of this round. The ninth mutation starts the distribution one token late, and it breaks every distribution pin of the sixth and seventh families and no other pin of this round. The tenth mutation removes the gate's three option vetoes together, and it breaks the root veto pin and the reconcile veto pin of the sixth family, and it leaves the form veto pin standing, because the distribution's own head check refuses the same tokens, and that standing is the demonstration of the joint coverage the census of FD-69 records. FD-131. The defects of a branch state are named in the order the reader reaches them. The second meaningful line is judged as the author line, so an interface line standing there is named as a missing author line.

FD-141. Census on 2026-11-16, the value equality the reconcile stage command reads was censused by mechanism in src/reconcile/pynumber.rs and by spelling across the standing reconcile corpus. The stage command compares the two states term by term. Two values whose normalized texts are identical stage as clean whatever they read as, and two values whose texts differ stage as clean only when both read as finite numbers of equal worth under the decimal grammar the Python conversion obeys and inside its exponent validity rules, so two spellings of one worth outside those rules stage as conflicting, as FD-102 records. The standing corpus of 230 cases holds exactly four stage cases that pair two spellings of one worth as clean, a double-struck zero against an ASCII zero, an underscored coefficient against its plain form, a signed zero against zero, and a trailing fraction zero against the integer it names. No standing case stages a value carrying a leading plus sign, no standing case stages a value in Arabic-Indic digits, and no standing case stages a coefficient of twenty digits or more on either side of any command.

FD-142. Six oracle cases join the reconcile corpus this round and each was baked against the release binary. The verdict of each case was also checked against the Python runtime decimal reading during the round, and the runtime agrees with all six, the five clean pairings reading as equal and the forty digit pair reading as different. The case stage-value-signed-clean pairs a timeout of +30 against 30 and stages the term clean. The case stage-value-dot-forms-clean pairs a limit of .5 against 0.5 and a timeout of 5. against 5 and stages both terms clean. The case stage-value-underscore-exponent-clean pairs a timeout of 1e1_0 against 10000000000 and stages the term clean. The case stage-value-arabic-digits-clean pairs a timeout written in Arabic-Indic digits against 30 and stages the term clean. The case stage-value-long-coefficient-conflicts pairs two limits of forty digits that differ only in the last digit and stages the term conflicting. The case stage-value-exponent-at-cap-clean pairs a limit of 1e999999999999999999 against 10e999999999999999998 and stages the term clean. The reconcile corpus moves from 230 cases to 236, the oracle count from 204 to 210, and the command line count stands at 26, and the command line corpus stands untouched at 111 cases.

FD-143. The equality is exact and canonical. Every underscore is removed before a value is read, the runtime whitespace set is stripped from both ends, and digits are read by their worth in any decimal alphabet the runtime admits, so the Arabic-Indic spelling and the ASCII spelling of one coefficient read as the same digits. A finite value is held by its sign, its coefficient digits with leading and trailing zeros removed, and the exponent those digits carry, and two values are equal when the three agree, with zero standing unsigned and equal to zero under every spelling. The digit string is never rounded and never converted at bounded precision, so a difference in the fortieth digit of a coefficient is a difference in worth and stages as a conflict. A written exponent is accumulated under a clamp and judged against the stored exponent floor and the adjusted exponent cap, and the two spellings at the cap are equal because the second coefficient carries one more digit and an exponent one lower, which the canonical form reads as the same sign, the same digits, and the same exponent.

FD-147. The cross-apply builds its name set from the two states' term and operation tables only. The label table, the excludes set, and the breaking set are read by the reader and never enter the classification, so a label definition moves no name into any list, a reported name defined only as a label stands missing because the missing list is the reported names less the name set, an excluded name classifies by its definitions alone, and a breaking mark changes no list a name lands in. The side forms a classification compares are the term form and the operation form, so a name defined as a term on one side and only as a label on the other holds one form on one side and none on the other and classifies one sided for the term side. The reader's own reading of the three kinds of line stands where FD-74 and FD-75, FD-85 to FD-87, and FD-133 to FD-136 pin it, and this round adds no refusal and moves no standing case.

FD-151 Six oracle cases join the command line corpus, each staging its malformed artifact on the left of a compare against the sound right artifact of the standing pair, each baked by execution against a binary built from this tree, and each answering on the error stream at exit code 2 with the report stream empty and the seeded files standing as seeded. compare-malformed-no-meaningful-line stages an artifact holding a comment line and a blank line and nothing more and answers that the artifact holds no meaningful line. compare-malformed-no-statement-form stages a plain line carrying no equals sign under a sound interface line and answers the no statement form refusal quoting the line. compare-malformed-bad-identifier stages a term line whose name is no valid identifier and answers the refusal naming that name. compare-malformed-empty-value stages a term line stating an empty value and answers the empty value refusal naming the term. compare-malformed-duplicate-term stages two term lines for one name and answers the duplicate term refusal at the second line. compare-malformed-self-exclusion stages an artifact defining a term its excludes line also names and answers the self exclusion refusal. Standing left for a sibling shapes round are the unquoted no statement form spelling behind a label or operation line carrying no equals sign, the duplicate operation and duplicate label spellings, the excludes line naming no valid identifier, the second excludes line, the source name line break refusal of FD-33, the right side placement of each fired shape, and the pairings of one side refusing against the other side refusing or the interfaces mismatching.

FD-152 The compare command reads and parses the left artifact before it reads the right, one read and one parse per side in argument order, on the reading of the code, and the pairings of one side refusing against the other side refusing or standing missing stand uncased as FD-151 records, so no case of this round discriminates the order across the two files and that order carries no pin. A refusal over the right artifact answers ahead of the comparison checks. The reader first gathers the meaningful lines, skipping blank lines and comment lines while counting line numbers over the physical lines, then judges the head, then the body line by line in order, then the closing self exclusion check over the excluded names in sorted order. The head refuses an artifact holding no meaningful line and then an artifact whose first meaningful line does not name an interface. A body line is taken as a label line by its label prefix, else as an operation line by its operation prefix, else as the excludes line where the text before its equals sign strips to the excludes word, else as a term line where it carries an equals sign, and a line taken by none of these answers the no statement form refusal quoting the line. A taken label, operation, or term line is split at its first equals sign, and the split judges in order the missing equals sign behind the unquoted no statement form spelling, the name against the identifier grammar behind the refusal naming the name, and the value against emptiness behind the empty value refusal, and a name already standing in its own table answers the duplicate refusal as its line is stored. The excludes line answers a second excludes line at that line ahead of reading its names, and each name the line carries is judged against the identifier grammar as the line is read. The comparison judges the source names for line breaks, the left name first, ahead of the interface equality check and ahead of every divergence family.

FD-153 Six mutation arms were run against the artifact reader, each an exact text substitution in src/artifacts.rs, each built and driven over the full command line corpus, and the source restored after each arm and verified by SHA-256. A reader that stores an empty value fails compare-malformed-empty-value alone, answering a term value divergence at exit code 1. A reader that stores a name already standing in its table fails compare-malformed-duplicate-term alone, the second value overwriting the first and the compare answering agreement at exit code 0. A reader that skips the closing self exclusion check fails compare-malformed-self-exclusion alone, answering excluded but defined and term value divergences at exit code 1. Every arm fails exactly the one case staged for its rule and no other case in the corpus, and every case of FD-151 stands in the failing set of its arm.

The compare command reads and parses the left artifact before it reads and parses the right, one read and one parse per side in argument order, and that order now stands pinned at both of its steps, at the read step by the standing case compare-negatives, whose two missing files answer for the left name, and at the parse step by compare-malformed-pair-left-first, whose two malformed artifacts answer with the left refusal, and the interleaving of the steps stands pinned by compare-malformed-left-beside-missing-right, which stages a malformed left artifact beside a missing right one and answers the left refusal because the left parse completes before the right read begins.

FD-167 The sweep command reads and parses the baseline before it reads any other, and the baseline refusal answers ahead of every other read, so a malformed baseline beside a missing other answers with the baseline refusal and not the read refusal. Each other is read and parsed and its pair compared in the order the others are given, and the first refusal in pair order is the refusal the sweep reports, whether it stands in a read or a parse or a comparison. The document prints only after every pair has been compared, so a refusal in a later pair leaves the document stream empty however the earlier pairs answered, and a diverging pair ahead of a refusing pair contributes nothing to the output. A refusal over an other carries that other source name and the text the same defect raises over a compare side, and the interface refusal inside a sweep names the baseline source and the other source as the pairwise refusal names them.

FD-175. On 2026-11-19 the reader refusal shapes were censused by mechanism across the standing corpus and across the code of the artifact reader, each staged artifact of the cli corpus classified by the refusal the reader would raise on it and each refusal counted by the position at which it answers. The reader raises twelve refusal shapes in a fixed order. The meaningful lines are gathered first and a text holding none refuses for holding no meaningful line. The head is judged next and a first meaningful line that does not name an interface refuses. The body is then walked line by line and a line matching no statement form refuses with the line quoted, a statement whose split finds no equals sign refuses without the line, a statement naming no valid identifier refuses, a statement stating an empty value refuses, and a name stored twice in one table refuses as a duplicate of its kind. An excludes line is judged as it is met and a second one refuses ahead of any reading of its names, a name on an excludes line naming no valid identifier refuses, and after the walk a name the artifact both defines and excludes refuses. Behind compare all twelve shapes answer on the left. Behind compare five shapes answer on the right, the no meaningful line shape, the duplicate term shape, the empty value shape, the formless shape in its bare spelling, and the self exclusion shape, and seven stand unfired there. Behind sweep two shapes answer at the baseline, the no meaningful line shape and the duplicate term shape, two answer at the first other, the duplicate term shape and the duplicate operation shape, and two answer at a later other, the empty value shape and the interface refusal of the comparison itself, the self exclusion shape standing staged at a later other in sweep-refusals-first-in-pair-order without answering. At the baseline ten shapes stand bare, and this round takes six of them, the head shape, the formless shape in its quoted spelling, the identifier shape, the duplicate label shape, the second excludes shape, and the self exclusion shape, one for each of six check sites of the reader. The formless shape in its bare spelling, the empty value shape, the duplicate operation shape, and the excludes identifier shape also stand bare at the baseline and stand left for a later round.

FD-176. Three cases fire three of the six shapes at the baseline of a sweep, each over the standing sound pair staged as the others. sweep-baseline-no-interface stages a baseline whose only line is a term line and answers the head refusal at line 1, the first meaningful line naming no interface. sweep-baseline-no-statement-form stages a baseline naming the standing interface and then a line of prose and answers the formless refusal at line 2 with the line quoted. sweep-baseline-bad-identifier stages a baseline naming the standing interface and then a term line whose name opens with a digit and answers the identifier refusal at line 2. Each case answers at exit 2 with the document stream empty and the seeded files standing as seeded.

FD-177. Each case answers at exit 2 with the document stream empty and the seeded files standing as seeded.

FD-178. The sweep command reads and parses the baseline before it reads the first other, and a baseline that refuses answers ahead of every other read and ahead of every pair comparison, so no pair header stands printed and the refusal is the whole answer. The six cases of this round stage two sound others behind the refusing baseline, so the answer each case records is the baseline refusal alone and no divergence of any pair stands behind it. The reader judges the baseline in the order it judges any artifact, the meaningful lines first, the head next, the body line by line in the order the lines stand, the excludes lines as they are met inside the walk, and the self exclusion check after the walk, and the six refusals of this round answer at their own check sites in that order. Behind a sound baseline the pair order of FD-167 governs the others, and nothing in this round moves it.

FD-179. The mutations were executed against the enlarged corpus and each stands restored. A reader whose head check is struck, so that a first meaningful line is taken as an interface line whatever it names, fails compare-malformed-left and sweep-baseline-no-interface. A reader that skips a formless line in the body walk fails compare-malformed-no-statement-form and sweep-baseline-no-statement-form. A statement split that stores a name without judging it fails compare-malformed-bad-identifier and sweep-baseline-bad-identifier. A label store that admits a name it already holds fails compare-malformed-duplicate-label and sweep-baseline-duplicate-label. A reader that admits a second excludes line fails compare-malformed-second-excludes, compare-malformed-second-excludes-bad-identifier, and sweep-baseline-second-excludes. A reader whose closing self exclusion check is struck fails compare-malformed-self-exclusion, compare-malformed-right-self-exclusion, and sweep-baseline-self-exclusion. A sweep that falls back to the first other when the baseline cannot be read or parsed fails sweep-baseline-no-interface, sweep-baseline-no-statement-form, sweep-baseline-bad-identifier, sweep-baseline-duplicate-label, sweep-baseline-second-excludes, sweep-baseline-self-exclusion, sweep-baseline-refusal-beside-missing-other, sweep-empty-baseline, and sweep-malformed-baseline. No arm fails a case of the reconcile corpus. Every case of this round stands in the failing set of the arm that strikes its own check and in the failing set of the fallback arm.
